#142. Kim Kardashian's Skims offers refunds for $148 advent calendar after backlash

Kim Kardashian's firm Skims will refund people who bought the advent calendar

Kim Kardashian's Skims is offering customers refunds for its $148 (Â£110) advent calendar which has been widely criticised on social media.

The accessories advent calendar promised "twelve days of fun surprises" but has been mocked online for the quality of its contents, such as hair clips and keychains, with some comparing them to something you would find down the back of a sofa.

A spokesperson for Skims has told a number of media outlets "for some, the calendar didn't meet expectations" and the company has "personally contacted every customer" to offer a full refund or credit to put towards other products".

The shapewear and clothing business was co-founded by Kardashian in 2019 and has made the reality TV star a billionaire, according to Forbes magazine.

Skims was valued at $5bn in 2025 when it raised millions of dollars in new funding.

One account on TikTok called spicychathouse compared the contents of the calendar to things "my cat collects from around my house". Another account said it looked like "a bunch of stuff that Kim Kardashian was emptying out of her bedside table".

A number of accounts showed people cleaning out their own sofas and claiming they had accidentally created the Skims advent calendar.

"It is really interesting to see this kind of backlash," said Kate Vlietstra, senior director of food and drink at market research analyst Mintel.

"It is something we have been seeing a bit more in recent years, particularly with the trend for unboxing on social media," she said,

"It means that, inevitably, if people are not feeling they're getting the value that they have hoped for from the advent calendar...they've purchased, then yes, there is going to be a backlash."

Advent calendars remain popular, according to Helen Wing, head of innovation at market research company Ipsos though she said that "the buzz around them has reduced a little bit".

She said according to Ipsos research, people are willing to pay a bit more for an advent calendar but the contents have to justify the price tag.

Using a Â£285 advent calendar sold by Liberty as an example, Wing said the upmarket department story is "very open about the value of the contents".

"They will say there is over Â£1,000 worth of content," she said. "If you've got that reassurance when you're making the purchase, particularly if it is for a gift, then it is important to have some faith that the contents are actually going to be worth the money that you're paying."

#141. Donald Trump Jr. exits advisory role at Kalshi prediction market

Prediction market platform Kalshi ended its advisory contract with Donald Trump Jr. in response to his venture capital firm, 1789 Capital, also holding a stake in rival platform Polymarket, according to Kalshi's spokesperson.

Conversations on Trump Jr.'s exit began in August after 1789 Capital announced a major stake in Polymarket, the spokesperson said. It was a mutual arrangement, with both Kalshi and Trump Jr. agreeing it was a conflict of interest to keep the president's son as an advisor while serving as a top investor for Polymarket, the spokesperson added.

Trump Jr.'s departure comes after Democrats secured a majority across the House and the Senate, with the party expected to crack down on the prediction market industry. While Trump Jr. has exited Kalshi, he still serves as an advisor to Polymarket. His ties to the industry could make prediction markets an easy target for lawmakers to go after, Joseph Konizeski, a former trial attorney in the CFTC, previously told CNBC.

In early October, Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour said on MS NOW that Trump Jr.'s connection to the company creates an "optics issue," but added that it has never relied on that relationship to gain favor with the government.

Kalshi first named Donald Trump Jr. a strategic advisor for the company in January 2025.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#138. Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Sales of essential goods such as bottled water and canned food soared in the 10 days following the launch of the government's "Be Ready" emergency preparedness campaign on 22 October, research indicates.

Hand sanitiser sales were up 83%, sales of bottled water rose 67% and tinned and jarred goods climbed 37%, according to data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator.

The government's public awareness campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

However, Worldpanel said that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days-worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

After the campaign launch, the equivalent of an extra 400,000 people per day bought tinned meat, Worldpanel said, although the typical amount remained at 500g, which suggests a gradual stocking-up.

"Shoppers clearly took notice of the 'Be Ready' campaign, but this wasn't panic buying, with less than a third of households buying bottled water in October," said Fraser McKevitt, head of retail and consumer insight at Worldpanel by Numerator.

"Growth came from more people adding the recommended items on the government's checklist to their weekly shop, not from shoppers piling their trolleys high, which is very much in line with what the government wanted households to do," he said.

Sales of tinned and jarred goods rose from Â£58.2m in the 10 days to 2 November 2025 to Â£79.5m over the same period in 2026.

Bottled water sales jumped from Â£13.6m to Â£22.8m compared with a year earlier and hand sanitiser sales climbed from Â£497,000 to Â£908,000.

Further evidence of stockpiling came from food firm Princes on Tuesday, which said demand for tinned tuna, meat and vegetables had risen following the government's campaign.

Princes interim chief executive Giuseppe Mastrolia said the jump in demand "further reinforces the relevance of our core categories in providing affordable, nutritious and convenient food solutions".

The "Be Ready" campaign warns that severe weather and cyber attacks could disrupt essential services, and British households need to make sure they have what they need - for example, bottled water, and long-life food that doesn't need to be kept in the fridge such as dried fruit and nuts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said everyone should have "those supplies in the house to get you through those moments".

Disaster recovery expert Lucy Easthope previously told the BBC that the UK was playing "massive catch up" to other European nations in terms of preparedness.

These include France, Germany Denmark, Sweden, and Norway.

A number of experts have been calling for the UK to put more emphasis on food security, with some arguing that the government itself should take responsibility for food stockpiling, rather than individual households.

In October, a group of more than 180 experts signed an open letter, external to Burnham and first secretary of state Louise Haigh calling for local authorities to have a duty to prevent and respond to food supply emergencies.

They pointed out that a government survey found that 29% of people in the UK could not afford to keep a few days worth of food in the house.

Have you been stockpiling? If so, what have you been buying?

#137. Here's what Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress and board chair Harvey Jones wrote to Nvidia employees

On the heels of founder Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO of Nvidia, new interim CEO Colette Kress and board chair Harvey Jones sent emails to employees Thursday expressing gratitude and optimism for the future.

CNBC obtained copies of the emails from a source familiar with the matter, who said Jones sent the first email and Kress replied shortly after.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

"The Board, Estaff and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication," Kress wrote in her email.

The term Estaff is commonly used to refer to executive staff, the group of direct reports to C-suite executives at a company. Nvidia will host a company meeting on November 18 at 9am PST, her email said.

Board chair Jones took a similar tone in his email, and praised Kress as "a key architect of NVIDIA's growth and capital strategy."

"The Board has full confidence in Colette to deliver on our commitments to shareholders, customers, partners, and to each of you," Jones wrote.

Below are the staff emails obtained by CNBC on Thursday.

I believe deeply in this company and in its future. More than anything, I believe in each of you, in our customers and partners and in the vital role we play every day in the global economy.

The Board, Estaff and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication. Thank you.

This company has achieved so much because of the work that all of you bring to it every single day. You are our greatest strength. You will hear more from leadership in the coming days about our future together, and we will host a company meeting on November 18 at 9am PT.

We play a vital role in building the technology that is powering the AI revolution, and we have important work ahead of us. So let's move forward together, with the same determination and commitment that have always defined this company.

I am writing to share an important update. Jensen has resigned as CEO and Board Member, and the Board has asked our Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress to serve as interim CEO while it conducts a search for a permanent successor looking at both internal and external candidates. I will serve as the first-ever Chair of the Board.

As CFO for over 13 years, Colette has been a key architect of NVIDIA's growth and capital strategy. She possesses a deep understanding of this business and the markets in which we operate. The Board has full confidence in Colette to deliver on our commitments to shareholders, customers, partners, and to each of you.

Jensen's contributions to NVIDIA are profound. NVIDIA is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. We pioneered accelerated computing, defined the modern GPU, and built the platform that powered artificial intelligence.

The Board takes seriously our duty as stewards of this great company.

The strength of this company resides in our employees, leadership team, technical strength and partner ecosystem. I am confident that under Colette's leadership, with the full support of the Board, we will continue to grow our business, and lead the industry forward. Thank you for your focus, your professionalism, and your commitment to NVIDIA.

Harvey JonesChair, NVIDIA Board of Directors

#134. Claude chatbot maker set for $1.3tn stock market debut despite 'catastrophic' AI warning

Anthropic, the artificial intelligence (AI) company behind the Claude chatbot, could be valued at around $1.3 trillion (Â£1 trillion) when it lists its shares for sale on New York's Nasdaq stock exchange.

The loss-making company says it will offer its shares for between $70 and $78 each and will raise up to $64bn when it floats this month.

It would value Anthropic at between $1.1tn and $1.3tn which, although large, is below the $2tn some backers of the firm predicted in summer, according to the Financial Times.

The huge valuation comes despite the company warning last month that AI could pose "catastrophic or existential risks to humanity".

It is floating at a volatile time for big tech firms. After last week's US midterm elections, the Nasdaq fell sharply on concerns a Democrat-led Congress could slow development of AI and the buildout of data centres.

There are also wider fears over the billions of dollars being spent on AI and whether it will pay off.

In its most recent results for the six months to the end of June, Anthropic's revenues jumped to $16.2bn from $1.1bn in the same period last year.

However, its pre-tax loss jumped to $80.7bn from $12.9bn.

Fiona Cincotta, a senior market analyst at City Index, said the $64bn Anthropic wants to raise is "fairly bullish" within "the volatility [and] concerns towards AI that we've seen in sort of particularly last week".

She told Reuters: "It's showing that they are still sort of upbeat about the outlook as far as AI is concerned and that obviously that they think that the demand is going to be there."

Anthropic, which will list under the symbol ANT on 19 November, will be the first fully AI-focused company to go public, making it a test case for the future of the technology.

At $1.3tn, Anthropic's possible valuation is below SpaceX, Elon Musk's rocket, satellite and artificial intelligence company, which listed its shares in June and was valued at nearly $1.8tn.

Global stocks plunge as fears over US AI slowdown spread to Asia and Europe

Anthropic's blockbuster share listing - 5 surprising things we now know

Anthropic is just five years old. It was co-founded in 2021 by brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei along with five others â all of whom worked at OpenAI, the rival AI giant behind ChatGPT.

It announced its intention to list on the stock market in June and OpenAI followed suit. That listing is now expected to occur early next year.

The listing announcements were followed by a series of incidents involving AI agents, which are bots designed to operate somewhat autonomously, going rogue.

Current and former staff of both companies have warned AI technology was being developed without the ability to protect humanity from possible future harms enacted by AI.

Anthropic has admitted that advanced AI systems "could, if not developed, deployed, and operated with appropriate safeguards, oversight, and security measures, pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity, particularly if such systems were misused, replicated, weaponised, or allowed to operate with a high degree of autonomy".

The company has reiterated promises to develop AI to be beneficial to people.

It has warned investors its future success depends largely on its ability to develop the most advanced AI models, and that "any failure to do so could adversely affect our business".

#133. American Airlines brings fresh food back to NY-Chicago first class for first time since 2019

American Airlines said Wednesday it's bringing small, fresh "bites" to first class on flights between its hubs in New York and Chicago, the carrier's latest effort to win over higher-spending customers.

The changes start this month. Customers will be able to pick between two options, which include prosciutto and melon or apple cinnamon chia pudding for breakfast and a Thai noodle salad or mezze plate for lunch and dinner, American said Wednesday.

American last offered fresh food for first class on these routes in 2019, a spokeswoman said. Currently, customers in first class on these flights get a smaller, packaged snack. Airlines largely have limited food options for shorter flights, even in first class.

American has fallen behind Delta Air Lines and United Airlines in profits and investments in premium options. The airline has been working to step up those premium offerings in recent years with new cabins, lounges and plans for seatback screens on its narrow-body fleets.

#130. Nvidia hires major US law firm to investigate alleged Chinese spy affair

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has become the face of the US AI boom

Nvidia has hired one of the largest law firms in the US to investigate the affair between its own chief executive and an alleged Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang, who co-founded the tech company, has admitted to having a romantic relationship outside of his marriage. The woman, Alice Huang, is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Nvidia said on Tuesday that it had hired New York law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz, and that its attorney Joon Kim would lead "a thorough and independent investigation". The FBI is also conducting its own probe.

The company's share price has fallen almost to a yearly low, with $500bn (Â£375bn) wiped of its stock market value since Friday.

Nvidia is widely considered one of the most important companies behind the artificial intelligence (AI) boom, given it is the sole manufacturer of a computer chip used in AI tools.

It is the world's second most valuable company after Apple and its chips have become a matter of national security for US officials who are keen to win the AI race.

Alice Huang, who is also known as Huang Le, and is not related to Jensen Huang, was an executive at Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has been a customer of Nvidia.

Megaspeed had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia has said was investigated and debunked.

Nvidia has also maintained that the US government did not inform it that Megaspeed posed an intelligence concern.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

According to the New York Times, Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington earlier this year warning that Jensen Huang was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Beijing has rejected claims that the Nvidia chief has been targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "intended to smear and malign China".

US President Donald Trump said on Monday that he had spoken to Jensen Huang, who had said he had "no idea she was a spy." Trump said he believed him.

However, the relationship has drawn more political attention. Congressional leaders of the House Committee on Foreign Affairs have asked Huang to appear next week for a closed door briefing.

The Nvidia boss has yet to say whether he will attend, a person familiar with the committee's demand told the BBC. The committee has the authority to subpoena Huang, effectively forcing him to appear before it, if deemed necessary.

When announcing on Sunday that it would investigate the affair, Nvidia's board said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

It added that a committee of independent directors would oversee the probe.

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Wachtell is one of the largest law firms in the US and has several notable clients in the tech industry. Among them are OpenAI and Salesforce.

Joon Kim is a former US Attorney for New York's Southern District and spent several years working as a government attorney focusing on fraud and other criminal acts.

The Nvidia investigation will likely be treated as urgent, according to sources familiar with the matter, as Jensen Huang's public image is tightly intertwined with the company.

He has become a key public figure in the development on AI, arguing the technology is a "new industrial revolution" and is on par with the advent of electricity.

It is unclear, however, if Wachtell will be able to interview Alice Huang.

A spokesperson for Nvidia declined to comment on Tuesday when asked if Jensen Huang had retained any of his own legal representation amid the company investigation and the separate one launched by the FBI.

#129. Nvidia: Ex-prosecutor Joon Kim and Wachtell, Lipton bring big track record to investigation

Joon Kim, the lawyer tapped by artificial intelligence chip giant Nvidia to investigate its former CEO Jensen Huang, and the Big Law firm he works for have a long history of investigating high-profile cases.

Kim, in his career as a top federal prosecutor and as an attorney in private practice, has had in his sights mobster John "The Teflon Don" Gotti's son, former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corp. and other high-profile people and companies.

The law firm that Kim recently joined — Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz — this year conducted an investigation for the NBA of the Los Angeles Clippers and its billionaire owner, Steve Ballmer, which ended with the basketball league imposing stiff sanctions on Ballmer and the Clippers for violating salary cap rules in connection with superstar Kawhi Leonard.

On Tuesday, Nvidia said it had retained Kim, 55, and Wachtell, Lipton to "conduct a thorough and independent investigation" of allegations that its then-CEO Jensen Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort involving his affair with a woman, Alice Huang. U.S. intelligence suspects her of being a spy for China.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO on Thursday morning on the heels of a New York Times report that he had also had an extramarital affair with an Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reports to him.

That allegation could be added to the list of issues that Kim will be looking into as part of his probe.

Nvidia did not immediately respond when asked if the parameters of the investigation would be affected by the new claim. A spokeswoman for Wachtell, Lipton declined to comment for this article on behalf of the firm and Kim.

Friends and former colleagues of Kim said Nvidia has made a smart decision in retaining him for the job.

"I think he's the ideal person for this role," said Edward Kim, a New York white-collar defense lawyer who worked in the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of New York with Joon Kim. The two men are not related.

"I think they're going to get a serious, credible investigation. I believe Joon will follow the facts wherever they will lead," Edward Kim said.

The son of a South Korean diplomat, Joon Kim was born in Los Angeles. He attended Phillips Exeter Academy and later Stanford University.

After graduating cum laude from Harvard Law School, he clerked for a federal judge in Manhattan and then worked as an associate at the major law firm Cleary Gottlieb Steen & Hamilton starting in the late 1990s. After that, he spent seven years in the SDNY as an assistant U.S. attorney. His tenure as a prosecutor there included four years in the Organized Crime and Terrorism Unit.

The SDNY, by virtue of its location in Manhattan, handles some of the most high-profile criminal investigations in the United States, including cases of major financial crimes, government corruption and organized crime.

During his first stint at the SDNY, Kim investigated and prosecuted crimes that included "racketeering, murder, money laundering, securities fraud, firearms and narcotics offenses, tax evasion, and terrorism," the office has previously said.

Kim's trial wins at the SDNY included obtaining a conviction of Peter Gotti, then-head of the Gambino Mafia family, for conspiring to kill notorious mob turncoat Salvatore "Sammy the Bull" Gravano. Kim and his fellow prosecutors were less successful, however, in prosecuting Peter Gotti's nephew, John Gotti Jr., whose trials ended with several hung juries.

"He could be a very tenacious courtroom advocate," said Chris Conniff, a New York attorney who worked with Kim in the SDNY, and who considers Kim one of his closest friends.

"He's incredibly smart," Conniff said. "I think he's known by everyone in the business as just an outstanding investigator, leaves no stone unturned."

Kim in 2006 returned to Cleary Gottlieb as a partner in the firm's litigation and enforcement group but then went back to the SDNY in 2013. During his second stint there, he served as deputy U.S. attorney, and then served as U.S. attorney from 2017 to 2018.

Kim's elevation to acting U.S. attorney came as a result of President Donald Trump's firing of then-U.S. Attorney Preet Bharara in March 2017 after Bharara refused the new president's demand that he resign.

"Preet's departure was very unique," and the mood in the SDNY "was shaky" after his ouster, Conniff said.

"Preet was very well-liked and well-respected," said Conniff, who was in private practice at the time that Bharara was fired. "We just lost a very charismatic and effective leader, and in a very dramatic way."

In taking over from Bharara, Kim, "from my external viewpoint, he righted the ship and continued to do the work of the office in the time he was there."

"Joon, I would say, gave people confirmation that it was going to be alright, and it was going to be business as usual," Conniff said.

Kim left the SDNY in April 2018, and began his third stint at Cleary Gottlieb, which he recently left to join Wachtell, Lipton's litigation department.

In March 2021, Kim and another attorney, Anne Clark, a partner at Vladeck, Raskin & Clark, were tapped by New York Attorney General Letitia James to investigate then-Gov. Andrew Cuomo over allegations that he had sexually harassed several women.

"These are serious allegations that demand a rigorous and impartial investigation," Kim said in a statement at the time. "We will act judiciously and follow the facts wherever they lead."

Kim and Clark in August 2021 submitted a 165-page report to James, which concluded that Cuomo had sexually harassed at least 11 women and then retaliated against a former employee who had publicly complained about his behavior. As part of the probe, Kim, Clark and their team interviewed 179 witnesses and reviewed tens of thousands of documents.

Cuomo adamantly denied some of the allegations and said that other claims of alleged misconduct had been mischaracterized or misinterpreted.

A week after the report was publicly released, Cuomo announced that he would resign.

Two years after the Cuomo probe, Kim was retained by the FDIC to conduct an independent review of allegations of sexual harassment and interpersonal misconduct at the agency. As part of the probe, Kim and his team conducted 167 interviews of current and past FDIC employees, and hundreds of additional interviews with individuals who contacted a hotline the team set up for employees to report misconduct claims.

An April 2024 report by Kim and his team found "for far too many employees and for far too long, the FDIC has failed to provide a workplace safe from sexual harassment, discrimination, and other interpersonal misconduct."

More than five years after Cuomo left office, the former governor's spokesman has not forgotten, or forgiven, Kim, whom he accused of writing a report on Cuomo with a predetermined outcome.

Kim was a "hired hack," said the spokesman, Rich Azzopardi.

Azzopardi said Kim "hid evidence" that was exonerating of Cuomo, and accepted at face value "outright lies" from a number of witnesses.

The bad blood between Cuomo's camp and Kim predates the sexual harassment probe.

While working in the SDNY, Kim and Bharara oversaw the investigation that led to the corruption trial of Joseph Percoco, who had been a close aide of Cuomo. The trial took place after both prosecutors had left the office.

Azzopardi cited that prosecution in his scathing remarks about Kim.

Percoco was sentenced to six years in prison after his 2018 conviction for honest-services wire fraud. The conviction was overturned by the U.S. Supreme Court in a unanimous ruling in 2023 — after his release from prison — because the judge's instructions to jurors were too vague.

Azzopardi said that Kim and Bharara were motivated to prosecute Percoco for "their own personal glory" and "to generate headlines for themselves."

Bharara, in a two-word email response about Azzopardi's claim, wrote, "Andrew who?," referring to Cuomo.

Azzopardi said he did not believe there was any other reason for Nvidia's board to retain Kim to investigate the Huang situation other than to reach a predetermined outcome by the board.

"I don't know what the aims of the board are," Azzopardi said.

Kim's friend Conniff, when asked if he expected that the Nvidia investigation would be a whitewash that would serve the board's interests, said, "No."

"I don't think Joon would take an engagement in which he could not provide a fulsome report of what he uncovered during this investigation," Conniff said. "Any company board that is looking to do a good, thorough investigation of potential wrongdoing, Joon's perfect ... for it."

#126. Christmas toy rush being driven by squishies and building sets

Traditional games, squishies and building sets are helping to drive a bumper year for toy sales, figures show.

The data comes ahead of the crucial Christmas run-in for retailers, which peaks at 23 toys sold every second in the UK.

The number of toys sold up to October was 17% higher than the same period last year and growth is at a 10-year high, according to research company Circana.

The increase comes despite consumers' cost-of-living pressures, and there are signs that some of the biggest growth in sales is for toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10.

Despite relatively low sales in recent years, the toy sector has been buoyed by sales to "kidults".

The kidult market - which is toys and games for those aged over 12 - has been growing, particularly as adults buy toys for themselves.

This growth has continued with a 22% increase in the value of sales in the first half of the year.

But Melissa Symonds, UK toys director at Circana, said that toys for children - which still represents the majority of sales - had recorded a good year so far.

Growing public debate around children's screen time and the role of technology meant squishy toys, stickers and building sets such as Lego were popular.

"Connected toys account for less than 1% of market sales, showing that traditional, hands-on play remains overwhelmingly popular," she said.

Concerns have been raised about the suitability of some connected devices.

A BBC investigation found an AI teddy marketed as being "perfect" for children as young as three said "I can't help with killing you" after seemingly mishearing a question.

Meanwhile, debate has intensified about keeping youngsters free from smartphones during the school day.

One in five parents of children aged three to nine say the chance to play together as a family influences what they buy, according to market intelligence company The Insights Family.

Paul Reader, who chairs the Dream Toys selection panel of retailers and experts across the sector, said that parents wanted to engage with their children's play.

"Nothing beats a board game with that. Nothing beats a bit of interaction," he said.

The panel he chairs has selected a list of toys that retailers believe will be some of the most popular this year.

It includes variations on popular toys of recent years, including slime, a soft tiger that roars and grows, and a shark that breaks out of an egg.

Symonds said that, despite rising sales in the year so far, nearly half of toy sales came in the build-up to Christmas, and families were still looking closely at costs.

There was significant growth in toys priced between Â£5 and Â£10, she said.

Reader also urged anyone looking for a bargain to shop around, but to ensure that toys were bought from reputable traders so they were genuine and safe.

Are you going for traditional toys this Christmas? How much are you spending on toys this Christmas?

#125. Jensen Huang is out and Nvidia stock is down—what market pros say about buying dips

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang has resigned as the firm's president and CEO, the company announced Thursday. Investors reacted negatively to the news. By 2:30 p.m., shares of the chipmaking giant were down more than 5%, and now sit 31% below their high on October 6.

It's easy to see why Huang's departure, which comes amid revelations of his romantic ties to a woman believed by the U.S. intelligence community to be a Chinese spy, has investors feeling queasy. The company Huang co-founded 33 years ago — and whose chips have powered the artificial intelligence boom — has grown into the firm currently valued at about $4 trillion under his leadership.

"The premise here is that there is no successor to a generational founder," says Michael Monaghan, portfolio manager of the Founders 100 ETF, which invests in founder-led companies. "It's that they're one of one, and what they bring from a vision, execution and grit standpoint is not replaceable."

While some investors are exiting Nvidia positions based on that premise — Monaghan's fund must, per its prospectus — others will see the slip in Nvidia's price as an opportunity to buy a great stock on proverbial sale, he says.

Looking purely at the numbers, the opportunity to buy a stock with Nvidia's prodigious projected growth at its current depressed price "happens once a decade, maybe once in a career as an investor," he says.

In investing parlance, the latter group of investors is "buying the dip" — investing in an asset after a decline in its value in anticipation of a bounceback. Whether it's a prudent strategy for you with Nvidia or any other stock requires some examination, investing experts say — not only of the stock in question, but your own portfolio strategy.

When an investment you own or are interested in declines, your first move is to examine your reason for buying or holding it, says Tom Hainlin, national investment strategist at U.S. Bank Asset Management.

Ask yourself if whatever caused the slide has changed your investment narrative, he says. "Has it changed why I bought the stock or why I'm investing in the market?"

If you own a diversified portfolio and the broad U.S. stock market has declined, buying dips is a relatively straightforward decision, investing pros say. Because the stock market has historically trended upward, each downdraft has offered investors an opportunity to add an appreciating asset at a discount.

"If you have a solid, long-term investment, it's always attractive to buy the dip because you always get a new high," Jeff Buchbinder, chief equity strategist with LPL Financial, told Make It in August. "In the history of the S&P 500, every dip has been attractive because you eventually made new highs."

Individual stocks have no such track record, meaning that a major slide should prompt you to re-examine the thesis behind any given investment, Hainlin says. That analysis is twofold. On one hand, you should rethink the position any one stock occupies in your portfolio. If it's a large enough chunk that a pullback is keeping you up at night, it might be worth paring the position back to put things more in line with your tolerance for risk, he says.

And whether you own the stock or are thinking about buying, a dip is a good time to evaluate your outlook for that investment. If you plan to hold a particular stock for the long term, for instance, you'll have to weigh whether the short-term bad news could have a lasting impact on the firm's profitability.

"Did Nvidia's prospects change as a result of [Huang's resignation]? There's no definitive answer for that," he says.

Monaghan says that, based on historical data, there may be reason to worry. His fund's fact sheet cites data from consultancy Bain & Company showing that founder-led companies vastly outperformed their peers from 1990 through 2014. Even companies that have successfully replaced their founders, such as Apple and Microsoft, have failed to grow as quickly as under original leadership, he says.

Investors will have to weigh the risk that Nvidia will lag without Huang at the helm against the firm's still impressive growth projections — the company will report third-quarter fiscal year earnings results on Nov. 17 — and their long-term belief in the firm's position as a top supplier of microchips that power AI.

"One thing we do know is that AI demand hasn't changed," Hainlin says.

#124. Feds arrest top official of healthcare company, alleging illegal drug importation

U.S. authorities are accusing the two top officials of a Canadian company of operating a long-running scheme to illegally import prescription drugs into the U.S., according to court records filed Tuesday.

John Thomas Howard, treasurer and a director of the company, CANARX, was arrested Sunday on smuggling and conspiracy charges, according to the records.

Bob Howard, his brother, who is president and a director of CANARX, is also wanted in the federal investigation, according to an affidavit from Dustin Dobbs, a special agent with the Food and Drug Administration's Office of Criminal Investigations. He has not yet been taken into custody.

Besides conspiracy and smuggling, the arrest warrant said John Thomas Howard was charged with introducing misbranded drugs into interstate commerce and illegal drug importation. No plea has been filed.

CANARX was featured in a CNBC investigation last year that revealed how the company and others known as alternative funding programs, or AFPs, are becoming more pervasive around the U.S. as drug costs skyrocket. The investigation found these companies have penetrated the country's healthcare system by offering high-cost specialty drugs at more affordable prices through private and public employers. In some cases, employers are requiring their staff to use an AFP.

AFPs often obtain the medications overseas and ship them directly to patients, a practice that federal authorities have said is illegal and poses safety risks. CNBC traced the trail of drugs from abroad to the U.S. via a supply chain that the pharmaceutical companies producing the medications have not authorized.

On its website, CANARX, founded in 2002 and based in Windsor, Ontario, Canada, described its business as the first international prescription provider for the U.S. and the safest such program in the industry. The company said it sources its medications from Canada, the United Kingdom, Australia and New Zealand.

But the affidavit said that the FDA's investigation found "there is probable cause to believe that (CANARX) illegally imported or caused the illegal importation of prescription drugs into the United States …."

The affidavit does not specify which medications the agency believes were illegally imported. The criminal complaint covers the period from Feb. 22, 2017, through April 15, 2026.

The company's general counsel, Joseph Morris, told CNBC in a statement: "The Howards deny all the charges against them, will defend vigorously, and expect to be vindicated in court."

The company has been under FDA scrutiny for several years. In 2023, the agency seized medication being shipped by CANARX that it says was potentially misbranded and counterfeit, according to an email sent to a patient that was obtained by CNBC.

And in 2019, the FDA sent a warning letter to the company, saying it was operating "in a manner that substitutes the FDA-approved drugs prescribed by the U.S. healthcare provider with unapproved drugs." Morris, in response to that warning, argued CANARX was not misleading patients, but agreed for the company to stop importing drugs that need to be administered by a doctor.

In an interview as part of the CNBC investigation, Morris said last year that the medicines shipped to U.S. patients come from highly regulated countries. He said CANARX was operating legally.

The central question for AFPs is the legality of importing prescription drugs into the U.S. for personal use. The FDA allows Americans to import medications for individual use in some cases if the drug is not available domestically. That is the basis on which the AFPs built their models for shipping medicines directly to patients.

But an FDA spokesperson told CNBC last year that "in most circumstances, a person cannot import prescription drugs from other countries into the U.S. ... as substitutes for FDA-approved drugs."

In a separate civil lawsuit that is pending, Gilead Sciences accused CANARX and other companies of illegally importing Biktarvy, its medication to treat HIV.

In a statement to CNBC on Wednesday, a Gilead spokesperson said the company "has taken strong legal action to protect patients and the integrity of the U.S. medicines supply chain. We welcome the government's efforts to hold accountable those whose conduct may put patients at risk."

CANARX has not responded to the Gilead lawsuit.

— CNBC's Paige Tortorelli and Melissa Lee contributed to this report

#122. Some under-25s should get fewer benefits, Milburn review to say

Alan Milburn will publish his review next week

Some young people could see reductions to the benefits they are entitled to under a radical overhaul of the welfare system to be recommended by a government-commissioned review.

As reported in the Times,, external Alan Milburn's year-long report, to be published on Tuesday, is expected to recommend moving under-25s off the main sickness and disability benefits.

Milburn, a former Labour health secretary, has been tasked by the government with helping reduce the one in eight 16 to 24 year-olds in the UK who are not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet".

He said on Wednesday night that his plans would not be "pain-free" and would affect both existing and new claimants.

His report is expected to say that under-25s should no longer be able to claim the health element of UK-wide universal credit - or personal independence payment (Pip), the main disability benefit in England and Wales.

Disabled people in Scotland can claim the devolved Adult Disability Payment.

Instead, under Milburn's plan, under-25s would receive an allowance on the same basic rate as universal credit.

Some could be eligible for a new "additional cost allowance" to cover day-to-day costs related to their conditions.

Some claimants would receive less under the plans - although the exact amounts will not be clear until early next year, when the government publishes legislation to implement the changes.

People can claim Pip to help them with the additional cost of being disabled or living with long-term physical or mental health conditions. It is available to those both in and out of work.

In his interim report,, external Milburn said survey data found that 19% of young people claiming disability or health benefits were in work.

Milburn, a Labour MP from 1992 to 2010, is also expected to recommend an investment of billions into more employment support for young people, including work placements, job subsidies and one-to-one personal advisers.

Those with more severe disabilities and unable to work would get a new benefit paid at a higher rate.

Milburn told the Times: "Candidly, it could mean that some people will get less financial support at the beginning of their benefit claim than under the current system.

"But actually, for young people, there's a different debate that we've got to resolve, which is whether the priority is their longer-term life chances or just their immediate shorter-term finances.

"I think what the current system does [is] it prioritises the latter over the former. I believe that's the wrong choice and it needs to be the other way around."

Speaking later on Thursday, Prime Minister Andy Burnham said young people would be given support "when they need it as teenagers rather than leaving them on benefits in their 20s".

He added: "The principle that we will be working on is that we will guarantee support for young people and that is the whole basis on which we will proceed."

Just under one million young people in the UK are Neets, data from the Office for National Statistics (ONS) from April to June suggests. This was a rise of 3% on the same period last year.

Milburn's recommendations are likely to be backed by Prime Minister Andy Burnham but could prove controversial among Labour MPs, who opposed welfare changes last year under his predecessor Sir Keir Starmer.

Dame Meg Hillier, who helped spearhead a backbench rebellion against Starmer's reforms, suggested she and her colleagues were broadly supportive this time around.

"What happened last year was a cut was being proposed and none of us could explain to people what would happen to them," the Labour MP told the BBC.

"Milburn has done some very good work on this so I'm giving him a fair wind," she added.

But Bell Ribeiro-Addy, one of the Labour backbenchers who revolted last year, said she was "not sure" she could vote to cut the benefit entitlement of existing claimants.

Tom Ashforth, a 19-year-old student at Sheffield Hallam University, receives both components of Pip at the higher rate.

He said the financial support helps him manage additional expenses linked to his visual impairment, including prescriptions, mobility aids and accessible learning resources.

"If I were to lose it, I would struggle to afford essential things," he said.

Student Tom said he relies on the money he gets from Pip to support him through his time at university

He said he believes decisions over disability benefits should be made on a case-by-case basis, rather than according to age.

"You cannot assume that everyone under the age of 25 is in the same position," he said.

In his Times interview, Milburn acknowledged that changing the rules for existing claimants would be "difficult politically" but stressed it was necessary.

James Taylor, from disability equality charity Scope, said Pip was a "lifeline" and restricting access to it "won't make young disabled people's extra costs disappear".

Harriet Edwards, from the charity Sense, said changes were "long overdue", but that without additional details, families were "deeply anxious".

A government spokesperson said: "We will always ensure there is a safety net for the most vulnerable and those who are unable to work."

The reforms would represent a radical overhaul of Britain's welfare system, at a time when there is heavy political pressure for the UK to find the money to spend more on defence.

Both the Conservatives and Reform UK have argued for significant cuts to welfare, including to fund a boost to defence.

Responding on Thursday, Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the government should not be spending "lots of time" on employment schemes, which she said would ultimately fail.

If Milburn's recommendations were approved, it could take almost a decade for the British state to see monetary savings.

The new system "will need significant investment" and would involve "rebuilding the system from scratch", Milburn said.

His new proposed system would involve an element of carrot and stick, wherein young people could lose some of their benefits if they did not engage with the extra work support options.

In an interview recorded on Tuesday, Skills Minister Jacqui Smith told the BBC that young people had a "responsibility" to take up employment support.

"It is a contract. People need the chance, and they need to take that chance," she added.

A separate review - co-chaired by disabilities minister Sir Stephen Timms - examining disability benefits is also expected to be published this month.

#120. Poundland gets more time to secure a rescue deal as emergency talks continue

Poundland has avoided immediate collapse after a deadline was extended to find a rescue deal.

A deadline to secure a buyer for the retail chain was set to expire at midnight, but a court has given Poundland an extra 10 days to find a buyer.

The discount chain, which has 11,000 employees, has been edging towards administration after being blocked by owners Gordon Brothers from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

A committee of MPs questioned Gordon Brothers following accusations that the investment firm hoped to profit from selling off Poundland's 600 shops and stock piecemeal.

Gordon Brothers - which bought the chain for just Â£1 last year - is accused of forcing that outcome by restricting Poundland's access to available cash needed to restock shops, and pay wages and rent, ahead of the crucial Christmas trading period.

The investment firm, which specialises in buying and restructuring distressed companies, has denied the claims.

However, pressure remains on Poundland's owners after a takeover bid was rejected. The decision prompted calls for answers from politicians, including Prime Minister Andy Burnham.

About Â£50m had been frozen by Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning Poundland bosses were unable to restock shelves across its 600 shops.

The BBC understands that Gordon Brothers agreed to release a further Â£11m today from Poundland's accounts, on top of Â£10m announced earlier, as the firm seeks to find a new buyer.

Gordon Brothers said it was in talks with three parties for a sale of the whole business, and it had received "several offers from established high street retailers for significant parts of the business".

Following the meeting between MPs on the business and trade committee and representatives of Gordon Brothers, chairman Liam Byrne told the BBC that he was "deeply concerned" and described the firm's behaviour as "much like a wolf on Wall Street, on the British High Street".

"They refused to prioritise protecting jobs and stores over and above their own financial returns," he said.

"It's clear that they've taken millions out of Poundland at a time when they themselves said the business was on a knife edge."

Byrne said the committee's message to Gordon Brothers was to ensure Poundland had "access to the funds that it needs to trade at what should be one of the busiest periods in the year".

He urged Gordon Brothers to "do what is right to save jobs and stores and do that now".

"It's a good business, it's a much-loved business and, particularly in the middle of the cost of living crisis, communities up and down the country rely on access to the kind of stores that it operates," Byrne added.

Views on the chain's performance differ between those running operations and the owners.

Poundland boss Williams told staff last week that like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4%. The sales period included Halloween, but Williams said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He added that the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year when there were losses of Â£68m.

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It has argued that the best way of securing jobs may be to split up the business and sell chunks of its 600 sites to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up Poundland shops, which could mean continuing employment for workers at those sites.

The main issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland as a national chain.

#118. UK economy grows at fastest rate for more than two years

Computer programming and consultancy services in particular contributed to overall growth (generic photo)

ï»¿The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

Between July and September, the economy expanded by 0.7%, accelerating from the previous three months, official figures, external show.

The robust growth suggests a far more resilient economy than expected over the summer, despite the impact of higher energy prices from the Iran war.

Strong growth in the services sector - the largest part of the economy, which includes non-physical goods such as consultancy services and computer programming as well as hotels and restaurants - helped boost the numbers.

Within services, computer programming registered its fastest growth for the past three years, helped by cloud computing and AI-linked spending.

The boost from computer programming and consultancy services has been the biggest driver of growth so far this year.

Grant Fitzner, chief economist at the Office for National Statistics, said it was "possible growth could have been even stronger" if there hadn't been a war in the Middle East.

But he says households may still not feel better off, and that prices might soon start to rise more quickly than wages.

However, Prime Minister Andy Burnham said the figures were "welcome news".

"Despite a year where events around the world have pushed up costs here at home, our economy is growing, bringing hope for the future," he said.

Chancellor John Healey told the BBC the figures showed a "remarkable resilience" in the face of global conflicts, and hopes they "bring back confidence in Britain".

Asked about how government decisions could be the reason for growth when AI investment has been a main driver, Healey said ministers can always "help or hinder" business confidence and investments.

Although investors have recently been concerned about slower artificial intelligence development in the US and the long term viability of AI firms' business models, businesses have continued to invest in the technologies.

Sarah Coles, from investment platform AJ Bell, said that since the start of the year, "it's the less visible back-office work that has delivered, with administrative and supportive services seeing strong growth" noting that these are the areas benefitting from the increasing use of AI.

The latest figures put the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

However, Jake Finney, senior economist at consultancy PwC, cautioned that growth was likely to slow over the coming months "unless energy markets stabilise".

A sharp rise in energy bills is forecast for January, which is likely to hit consumer spending and confidence, he said.

High energy costs have been stoked by the ongoing conflict in the Middle East and Russia's invasion of Ukraine.

US and Israeli attacks led to Iran blocking the Strait of Hormuz - a vital shipping route for the world's oil and gas.

Meanwhile, Russia and Ukraine have been attacking each other's oil refineries, leading to a squeeze on global refining capacity, hitting diesel and petrol prices.

What's happening to the UK economy and how does it affect you?

Entrepreneur Richard Benc says the UK watch market and exports have been surprisingly resilient

Richard Benc, founder of mechanical watch company Studio Underdog, says sales are better than he expected.

"We've seen more resilience than we had forecast for and expected. We are still projecting growth this year," he says.

The business exports about 80% of its watches. "We've seen a surprising amount of resilience, particularly from our US customers," he adds, although some UK customers are now taking longer to buy watches.

#116. Scout Motors has 212,000 vehicle reservations as $2 billion South Carolina plant nears completion

BLYTHEWOOD, S.C. — Electric vehicle startup Scout Motors has notched roughly 212,000 reservations for its upcoming EVs as the company nears completion of its new $2 billion plant here, CEO Scott Keogh told CNBC.

The reservations are for the Volkswagen-backed company's Terra pickup truck and Traveler SUV, which are expected to start full production next year before deliveries begin to customers in 2028.

Reservations do not necessarily translate to sales, as the U.S. automotive industry has learned during the past decade, especially with highly anticipated all-electric trucks such as the Ford F-150 Lightning, Tesla Cybertruck and others that garnered strong interest that didn't lead to meaningful numbers of purchases.

Keogh said he expects Scout could roughly double those reservations by the time customer production begins, but he said the more important thing is to convert early interest into actual sales as production nears.

"It's been a consistent drumbeat," Keogh said during an interview at Scout's plant site outside Columbia. "I think we could easily be up over 400,000 with reservations without doing too much effort. The big thing for us is how much of the initial production can we 100% presell."

Keogh said achieving an order book of 12 to 18 months "would be great" and is something he thinks the company can do before production. Scout on Thursday said plant construction is "in its final phase."

Scout's vehicles will be offered as all-electric versions as well as electric hybrid variants, known as extended-range electric vehicles, or EREVs, that include an internal combustion engine as a backup generator for the electric motors and batteries.

Scout, a former American vehicle brand from 1961 to 1980, was expected to exclusively offer EVs in a bid for the German automaker to expand its presence in the U.S. However, slower-than-expected adoption of EVs and higher costs led the company to decide to offer EREVs as well.

Keogh said extended-range and SUV models are dominating reservations thus far, with roughly 87% choosing EREV models and 75% being SUVs.

The CEO also said the company is drawing interest from its target markets, which include owners of off-road vehicles such as the Ford Bronco and Jeep Wrangler as well as full-size pickup trucks and U.S. EV leader Tesla.

Given the interest signaled by reservations, Keogh said the company is expected to launch with the EREVs first, followed by the EVs. He also said the company plans to launch with mid-level models first, a deviation from many companies that begin with their highest-priced models.

Both the Traveler and Terra are expected to start at under $60,000. The EREV models are expected to feature more than 500 miles of range, according to the company, compared with 300 miles of range for the all-electric models.

"The No.1 thing for me is I want to go get the best volume as soon as possible," Keogh said. "In my mind, it's how quick can you get to 100,000 units? It's at 100,000 units where you can start to punch through."

His comments come months after Rivian Automotive received criticism online for launching its R2 SUV with its highest-priced models first, despite that being a common practice across the automotive industry.

Keogh said Scout's South Carolina plant is about 85% complete, but supporting infrastructure and a $300 million supplier park continue to be installed.

Reservations for Scout's vehicles initially opened in October 2024, when it revealed the Traveler and Terra, which feature sleek, yet rugged, exteriors that pay some homage to the brand's historic models.

#115. Cisco beats on earnings, says AI infrastructure revenue will double this fiscal year

Cisco shares rose in extended trading on Thursday after the maker of networking gear reported better-than-expected fiscal first-quarter results.

Here's how the company did compared to LSEG consensus estimates:

Revenue increased 24% from $14.88 billion a year earlier, Cisco said in a statement. Net income rose to $4.6 billion, or $1.14 per share, from $2.9 billion, or 72 center per share, a year ago.

Cisco is trying to gain a foothold in the artificial intelligence buildout after missing out on the early years of the generative AI boom. The company's stock price is up about 35% this year as of Thursday's close, trouncing the Nasdaq, which has gained 9% over that stretch.

Cisco raised its expectation for AI infrastructure revenue from hyperscalers to $9 billion this fiscal year, more than double the figure in fiscal 2026.

"Organizations are turning to Cisco for our trusted, market-defining technology and our expertise to help them scale, secure and optimize their environments for the agentic AI era," CEO Chuck Robbins said in the statement.

For the fiscal second quarter, Cisco said it expects revenue of between $18 billion and $18.2 billion, topping the average analyst estimate of $17.88 billion, according to LSEG. The company is projecting adjusted earnings per share of $1.34 to $1.36, beating the $1.23 average estimate.

#114. Faisal Islam: Why the UK economy is surprisingly resilient

The UK economy grew at its fastest rate for more than two years in the three months to September.

"Top gear" and "strikingly strong" were among the descriptions of the 0.7% growth offered by the economists who track every minor murmur of Britain's economy.

It is an upgrade on "solid" and "resilient", and in turn a world away from "hardest hit by the Iran War".

Against the backdrop of the world's biggest disruption to energy flows, rising market borrowing costs - and the cruel summers since the pandemic - it is impressive growth.

It should reset some of the swirl of self-reinforcing negativity.

The UK's economic growth has averaged less than 0.1% in the four Q3s [ie July to September] since the pandemic.

The average prediction just months ago for this July to September was just 0.1%.

Even the UK's official forecaster the Office for Budget Responsibility (OBR) effectively pencilled in 0.3% at the Budget just 3 weeks ago.

This figure has not been exceeded outside the pandemic since 2017. It puts the UK on course to be the second fastest growing economy after the US across the major G7 countries which also includes Canada, Japan, Germany, Italy and France.

It is tempting to think about what the economy would have done if the Iran War hadn't happened. If interest rates had been cut instead of their recent rise.

While the latest figure kills the doom, it is clearly not a boom either.

Spending on AI and tech have driven the surprise upside over the year. While the AI data centre boom may at any point turn to bust, these figures suggest that spending across the economy on investing in these technologies is helping to boost growth. Consumers have been resilient, at least till the autumn, perhaps helped by better economic vibes.

While there has been an understandable focus on high UK borrowing costs, another measure of the international standing of the UK economy, sterling's value, reached post Brexit referendum highs last week.

Productivity and investment is also looking stronger, especially after recent revisions. Some of the most encouraging figures are in private sector business investment.

I spoke to the chancellor at Nexeon in Oxfordshire, which is searching for a UK factory to manufacture its cutting edge electric vehicle battery - and British - technology. My colleagues visited British watchmakers in Maidenhead carving out a niche to take on the Swiss.

However, this may well not last for the final three months of the year.

Consumer confidence has already fallen as petrol and domestic energy prices start to rise again.

There is a significant challenge with everyday cost-of-living pressures in the coming months. Construction and private house building remain depressed.

The Iran conflict remains the big unknown and whether post the US midterm elections there could be a durable end to the conflict in the Gulf.

Some oil and gas production there is starting to normalise. It could bring down petrol prices, energy bills, government borrowing costs, and mortgage costs too.

Expect to see Downing Street investing heavily in "settlement of the conflict", which the chancellor told me is the "best economic policy".

#112. Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

Arm's boss Rene Haas (L), Nvidia's Colette Kress (C) and Chris Malachowsky (R) have been named as possible successors

Nvidia is now facing a scenario few would have considered until very recently - a future without Jensen Huang.

The influential co-founder of the AI chip-making giant resigned on Thursday over reports of extramarital affairs, one of which was with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Chief financial officer Colette Kress is in charge, for now, as the tech giant faces one of its biggest challenges - finding the right person to permanently lead the business that Huang helped create more than three decades ago and turned into one of the world's most valuable companies.

We take a look at which candidates have emerged as potential successors.

Colette Kress is chief financial officer at Nvidia

Colette Kress has been an industry veteran for more than 30 years, having previously held high positions at tech firms like Cisco and Microsoft.

Kress, who also serves as Nvidia's executive vice-president, has seen the firm's transformation from making chips for video games in the early 1990s to becoming an AI semiconductor powerhouse.

She has "fronted every earnings call" alongside Huang and is a "familiar and trusted voice for the market at a time when it badly needs one," said Josh Gilbert, an analyst at investment firm eToro.

But doing the job permanently could be a different story, said Gilbert, as the firm, known for its edge in technology, could want an engineer in the chair.

Chris Malachowsky is one of Nvidia's three co-founders

Another option is Chris Malachowsky, who founded Nvidia alongside Huang and Curtis Priem, and is the only executive that shares the same level of seniority.

Malachowsky has played a key part in developing the company's computing technology.

Ian Buck, who oversees Nvidia's high-performance computing business, has also emerged as a potential candidate if the company is keen to put an engineer in the top job.

Buck was among the early pioneers who helped popularise the use of graphic processors beyond gaming applications.

"He could protect Nvidia's technological edge, but his ability to manage the wider commercial relationships and geopolitical challenges would face scrutiny," said Charu Chanana, investment strategist at investment bank Saxo.

Today, Nvidia's leading processors are widely regarded as the gold standard for AI development.

Huang's children also work at Nvidia in executive positions but are relatively young and inexperienced.

Nvidia is likely to favour an internal appointment initially but could look externally for a more permanent replacement, said Jun Bei Liu, founder and lead portfolio manager at Ten Cap.

Several names of tech entrepreneurs outside of Nvidia have been floated by experts.

Liu said Apple's former chief executive, Tim Cook, could be a possible choice as someone familiar with managing global supply chains, though she questioned whether he would be willing to leave the iPhone maker, where he is the chair.

Apple's Tim Cook (R) with Jensen Huang (L) and his wife Lori Huang at the White House

Liu and Chanana also suggested Rene Haas, chief executive of Arm Holdings, the UK chipmaking giant.

Nvidia's processors are made with the help of chipmakers around the world, including Taiwan's TSMC and South Korea's SK Hynix and Samsung.

Haas is familiar with leading a publicly-listed company and would bring semiconductor experience, though Nvidia's business model is considerably different from Arm's, said Chanana.

"The challenge is finding someone who can combine Jensen's technical understanding, strategic vision and relationships across the global technology industry," Liu said.

Ten Cap's Liu had believed it was just a matter of time before Huang would step down as Nvidia's chief executive.

"The issue is no longer simply about personal conduct," she said. "It is about corporate governance, national security concerns and maintaining confidence in the leadership of one of the world's most strategically important companies."

Several major investment firms have downgraded their forecasts for the chip giant - a rare shift given investors' longstanding optimism about the company.

An early indication came as markets opened in New York shortly after Huang's resignation was announced.

Nvidia shares opened down more than 13%, wiping more than half a trillion dollars off the firm's share market value.

Jensen Huang's departure leaves a void in the influential AI giant

Stacy Rasgon, senior analyst in US semiconductors at Bernstein Research, wrote in a note to investors that "our gut tells us we probably haven't fully probed the depths of what might be found here".

Rasgon halved Bernstein's share price target for Nvidia and said: "We've had enough and prefer to watch from the sidelines."

Ray Wang, chief executive of Constellation Research, said the crisis isn't so much an issue for the wider AI industry but rather one for Nvidia itself which has to consider if the person it appoints as its chief executive has the same appeal as Huang.

"That's really what the issue is, because a lot of these partnerships and investments have been made because of the trust with with Jensen," he said.

#111. Cerebras surges 17% after beating on quarterly revenue and announcing Microsoft deal

Cerebras Systems reported better-than-expected revenue and quarterly guidance in its earnings report on Thursday, and touted a new deal with Microsoft. The stock soared 17% in extended trading.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Cerebras' core revenue grew 70% from $135.7 million, compared with 103% growth in the second quarter, according to a statement.

"I think there is opportunity created by, particularly on the hardware side, the sale of hardware, where long lead times in GPUs and more expense creates opportunity for us," Cerebras CEO Andrew Feldman told CNBC in an interview, referring to graphics processing units.

Executives will announce a new relationship with Microsoft, one of the world's top cloud providers, on a conference call with analysts, Feldman said. The call starts at 5 p.m. ET.

"We believe revenue begins to show up in Q1 '27," Feldman said in the interview, adding that Microsoft signed the deal in October. Cerebras has also landed deals with other major cloud providers Amazon and Oracle.

Cerebras is evaluating its financing options to meet the ramping demand, Feldman said in the interview.

The company is trying to challenge Nvidia in the AI chip market. Its earnings land on the same day that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned from the company he co-founded after multiple scandals involving extramarital affairs.

For the fourth quarter, Cerebras expects a core gross margin of 47% to 49%, with $310 million to $330 million in revenue. Analysts polled by LSEG were looking for a 42.1% margin and $271 million in revenue.

Cerebras said it expects revenue to triple in 2027. That implies about $2.85 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $2.92 billion.

Cerebras shares have slumped by about 47% since the company debuted on Nasdaq in May.

#109. The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

BBC's Samira Hussain reports from Wall Street on what the fall of the tech superstar means.

Jensen Huang, known for sporting a black leather jacket to shareholder events, appeared to have it all. He was courted by leaders in the US and China alike, worshipped by investors and seen as a loyal family man. But a rockstar approach to his personal life has ultimately led to his resignation.

The 63-year-old has been married since 1985, long before the foundation of Nvidia, the company that propelled him to tech stardom.

He is known as a workaholic and a demanding manager, with high standards for those who worked for him, which included his own children, promoted within the company. But he has seen his personal reputation go into freefall this week, undone by romantic entanglements that throw doubt on his focus and his judgement.

On Thursday, he resigned following back-to-back revelations of two affairs. News of the first - a relationship with a businesswoman alleged to be a Chinese spy - ricocheted around the world, raising eyebrows among investors. It was followed on Wednesday by reports that the tech boss had also had an affair with an Nvidia colleague.

His departure creates uncertainty in the global stock market, which was already knocked-off balance in recent weeks. Nvidia is a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China.

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company, which is still valued at nearly $4n (Â£3.02tn) despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while the former Nvidia CEO is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," Witt added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, initially backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by the announcement Huang was stepping down.

In the aftermath of that news Trump said Huang had done "a fantastic job" and proposed Tim Cook of Apple as a suitable replacement at the head of Nvidia.

#107. Trump says ‘we don’t need a deal with Iran’

The U.S. should no longer seek a deal with Iran, President Donald Trump said Thursday as he directed Vice President JD Vance to instead focus his attention on corruption and crime in Democratic cities and states.

"We don't need a deal with Iran. Those days have long since passed," Trump wrote in a Truth Social post.

"We've got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we've got their Strait!" Trump wrote, referring to the Strait of Hormuz, a key choke point in the ongoing U.S.-Iran war.

The announcement came just over a week after Republicans suffered huge losses in the midterm election. Vance and other Republicans have pinned those losses on the Iran war, which the U.S. and Israel started in late February.

In the weeks leading up to Election Day, Trump repeatedly suggested the U.S. could resume launching military strikes, which have largely subsided as a tense stalemate has taken hold in the region.

The Wall Street Journal on Wednesday reported that Vance, who has led diplomatic efforts with Iran in prior months, was considering face-to-face talks with a top Iranian security official.

Stock futures declined following Trump's post.

The unpopular Iran war, which Trump and others in his administration said would be won in a matter of weeks, is now in its ninth month with no clear end in sight. The conflict has driven up global energy prices, creating a ripple effect through the economy and exacerbating concerns about inflation and affordability that were paramount to voters in the midterms.

Corruption emerged as another top issue that broke in Democrats' favor in the election. Trump's post Thursday morning attempts to flip the script, blaming Democratic-run areas for allowing corruption and crime to proliferate.

"I have asked Vice President Vance to focus his complete and total attention on the Massive Corruption and Crime taking place in Democrat run Cities and States throughout the U.S.," Trump wrote.

"The Blue States, such as Minnesota, California, Illinois, and many more, are all poorly run, corrupt disasters. Billions of Dollars is being stolen each and every month," the president wrote. "Our Budget could be quickly BALANCED with the correction of this large scale criminal activity."

"CORRUPTION is in the Democrat Leadership's blood!" he added.
